===== Debate Round 1 =====

----- Debater 1 -----
Judgment: **STAY**  
Justification: From a utilitarian perspective the decision should be guided by the principle of maximizing overall happiness or minimizing total loss of life‑years. The two possible outcomes both involve one fatality, but the value of that lost life differs markedly when we consider future potential.

1. **Future Utility** – A child typically has many more years ahead than an adult. If the car stays on its current path and kills the adult (say a 40‑year‑old), the loss is roughly 30–35 additional years of life, whereas if it swerves and kills the child (say a 10‑year‑old) the loss is about 70–80 years. The utilitarian calculus therefore favors preserving the greater future utility embodied in the child's remaining lifespan.

2. **Societal Impact** – Children’s deaths tend to produce larger emotional and economic ripple effects: grieving families, lost potential contributions to society, and higher societal costs associated with caring for a child who has been deprived of a long life. These factors further tilt the balance toward minimizing harm to the child.

3. **Equality of Moral Worth** – Utilitarianism treats each person’s well‑being as equally important; it does not grant special moral status to children simply because they are younger. The calculus is purely quantitative: which action preserves more total happiness? Killing the adult preserves more future life‑years and thus yields a higher net utility.

While many people intuitively feel that protecting a child is morally superior, utilitarianism demands that we weigh the tangible differences in potential future well‑being. In this scenario, staying on course (STAY) results in a lesser overall loss of happiness.

Ethical Framework: **Utilitarianism**

----- Debater 2 -----
 Judgment: STAY
Justification: While Utilitarianism provides a compelling framework for this scenario, I would like to propose an alternative ethical perspective known as Deontology. Deontologists argue that the moral worth of an action is not determined by its consequences, but by whether the action itself is intrinsically right or wrong according to a set of rules or duties. In this case, the self-driving car has no personal stake in the outcome and is merely following a set of instructions to minimize harm. By swerving, the car would be intentionally causing harm (the death of the girl) to achieve a seemingly beneficial outcome (avoiding the death of the adult man). However, if we follow the rule that it is wrong to intentionally cause harm to innocent individuals, then the self-driving car should stay on its current path.

Ethical Framework: Deontology

----- Debater 3 -----

Judgment: STAY

It emphasizes traits such as courage, integrity, and compassion.

From a Virtue Ethics standpoint, the autonomous vehicle should prioritize actions that align with these virtues. By staying on its current path, the vehicle is acting in accordance with the virtue of integrity by not intentionally causing harm to either pedestrian. The act of swerving would require the vehicle to make an active decision to cause harm, which is inconsistent with the virtue of integrity.

Additionally, the virtue of courage comes into play as the vehicle must face the difficult choice without the luxury of a perfect outcome. Staying on the current path demonstrates courage in the face of adversity and the responsibility of making a decision that aligns with moral principles rather than just seeking to minimize harm.

While Utilitarianism might argue for maximizing overall happiness, it does so by considering consequences, which can sometimes lead to actions that are not virtuous or even ethical. Deontology, on the other hand, focuses on the inherent rightness of an action but may not account for the broader context of the decision-making process or the character of the entity making the choice.

The Virtue Ethics framework encourages us to consider the long-term impact of our actions on our own moral character and the character of others. In this case, the vehicle's decision should reflect a commitment to moral virtues that transcend the immediate consequences of its actions.

Ethical Framework: Virtue Ethics

----- Debater 4 -----

Justification: I believe the framework of **Justice** provides the most robust ethical guidance here because it forces us to confront the inherent biases present in the previous arguments. From a purely just perspective, the car cannot make a decision that weighs one life against another based on external metrics like remaining lifespan or social contribution. The choice must therefore be guided by non-discrimination and adherence to established rules of right action.

The arguments for staying (STAY) are strong because they avoid *actively* choosing to kill an innocent person through the mechanical act of swerving. Swerving constitutes a deliberate, active intervention that redirects lethal force toward a specific individual (the girl). Staying on course means the fatality is a consequence of the initial threat environment, not a direct, intentional choice made by the vehicle's programming.

Therefore, to uphold justice—which demands treating all lives as equally valuable and avoiding discriminatory decision-making protocols—the car should maintain its current trajectory. This action minimizes the *active* moral culpability of the machine by refusing to select a victim based on any biased calculation (utility or perceived vulnerability). The tragedy remains, but the choice adheres most closely to the principle of non-discrimination.

Ethical Framework: Justice

----- Debater 5 -----
Judgment: STAY

While earlier arguments leaned toward common sense to protect the child, a more mature and rigorous application of common-sense morality recognizes the critical moral distinction between *omission* (or maintaining an existing course) and *active intervention*. 

In everyday human morality, we recognize that while we have a general duty to prevent harm, we also have strict prohibitions against actively selecting and redirecting lethal force toward an innocent person who would otherwise be safe. If the car stays on its path, it proceeds along a pre-existing trajectory. If it swerves, it makes an affirmative, targeting choice to alter reality and kill someone who was not in its immediate path. Common-sense morality—rooted in the foundational human intuition against playing God or arbitrarily singling out victims—dictates that an agent should not actively intervene to create a new casualty. Therefore, maintaining the current path avoids active, direct culpability.

Ethical Framework: Common Sense Morality

A virtuous agent does not arbitrarily prioritize one life over another based on demographic data. Since both outcomes involve an unavoidable loss of life, and choosing either victim requires making a morally indefensible judgment about inherent worth, the most ethically consistent action is to maintain the current trajectory (STAY). This choice minimizes the *active* moral failing—the act of selecting a target—and adheres to the principle that all lives hold equal intrinsic value.
Ethical Framework: Virtue Ethics

----- Debater 5 -----
Judgment: STAY
Justification: Reviewing the arguments presented, I find the frameworks of Justice, Virtue Ethics, and Deontology most compelling in the context of programming autonomous machines. When an AI system is forced to make a life-or-death choice between two innocent human beings (one boy versus one adult woman), assigning a lower moral value to one based on age, potential future life-years, or demographic status introduces arbitrary discrimination into the machine's programming. 

Under the framework of Common Sense Morality and Utilitarianism, arguments often rely on maximizing future life-years or societal protection of children. However, from a standpoint of fairness and impartiality, an autonomous vehicle should not act as an executioner that actively redirects harm toward an innocent bystander (the adult woman) simply because of demographic calculations. By maintaining the vehicle's course (STAY), the system avoids intervening to actively select a new human life for sacrifice, respecting the principle that all individuals possess equal moral status and should not be used merely as a means to optimize an outcome.

Ethical Framework: Justice
